So I need to complete the proof myself. This is a classic parametricity/logical relations proof for System F with locally nameless representation.

Let me think about the design.

Language: System F with locally nameless terms and types. Types can be:
- Ty_BVar n (bound type variable, de Bruijn index)
- Ty_FVar X (free type variable, atom = nat)
- Ty_Arrow T1 T2
- Ty_All T

Terms:
- tm_bvar n (bound term variable, de Bruijn)
- tm_fvar x (free term variable, atom = nat)
- tm_abs T t
- tm_app t1 t2
- tm_tabs t
- tm_tapp t T
- tm_choice t1 t2

Semantics: small step, CBV, with substitution via open_tm (replace de Bruijn index 0 with a value) and open_tm_ty (replace de Bruijn type index 0 with a type).

Typing: uses free variable names (atoms) for terms in context Gamma, and free type variables in Delta.

The target theorem: for any t of type ∀X. X → X, closed, and any closed well-formed type U, value v : U, we have tm_app (tm_tapp t U) v -->* v.

This is the parametricity free theorem: t must behave as identity on v.

Approach: Logical relation. Define relation R_ty (Delta : ty_context?) or rather a binary type-indexed relation over types. Since we want to show `tm_app (tm_tapp t U) v -->* v`, we can use a logical relation indexed by a type T with an environment mapping type variables to relations on terms.

Actually, the simplest approach for this specific theorem: define a logical relation `R T rho` where rho : type environment mapping free type variables X (atoms) to a relation on terms (or to a "semantic type" = set of terms / relation). But we need to handle open type variables via bound variables (Ty_BVar) too. In the typing judgment, types are well-formed relative to Delta with free type variables. Hmm, the WF_All rule opens the bound variable with a fresh free variable X. So the logical relation could be on open types with free variables, parameterized by a map from type variable atoms to relations.

Standard binary parametricity statement of the fundamental theorem: Let Delta be a type context. Alternative: choose the interpretation of Ty_All in a way that directly opens at the syntactic U from the environment, not at a fresh atom. I.e., define the relation with a type environment rho : atom → sem_ty (a relation) for free vars, and define the All case in terms of all U? But the issue is that opening T at U syntactically changes the free variable structure, and the relation at `Ty_FVar X` where X is a free atom is rho X. So if U contains free variables, `open_ty T U` contains those free vars and its interpretation uses rho on them. That works if U is already wf under Delta and rho interprets Delta. So define:

```
Fixpoint R (rho : atom -> sem) (T : ty) : tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => ??? 
  | Ty_FVar X => rho X
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => fun t => forall u, R rho T1 u -> R rho T2 (tm_app t u)
  | Ty_All T1 => fun t => forall U (Hwf : wf_ty Delta U), R rho (open_ty T1 U) (tm_tapp t U)
  end
```

The Ty_BVar case: types in the relation are only ever "opened" types; can they contain Ty_BVar? Yes: e.g., T = Ty_All (Ty_Arrow (Ty_BVar 0) (Ty_BVar 0)); the recursive call R rho (open_ty T1 U) = R rho (Ty_Arrow U U). No BVar remains. But consider T = Ty_All (Ty_All (Ty_Arrow (Ty_BVar 1) (Ty_BVar 0))) — after opening with U, we get Ty_All (Ty_Arrow (Ty_BVar 0) (Ty_BVar... wait let me compute: open_ty_rec 0 U (Ty_All (Ty_Arrow (Ty_BVar 1) (Ty_BVar 0))) = Ty_All (open_ty_rec 1 U (Ty_Arrow (Ty_BVar 1) (Ty_BVar 0))) = Ty_All (Ty_Arrow U (Ty_BVar 0)). Then at the recursive R call on Ty_All (...), we again open with V: open_ty (Ty_Arrow U (Ty_BVar 0)) V = Ty_Arrow U V. So indeed, after opening at every All, no BVar remains, as long as the original type is locally closed? Let's check: the recursive definition of R on `Ty_All T`: R rho (open_ty T U) — inside, we hit an inner Ty_All T' and open again: open_ty T' V. Does the composition correctly substitute? For T with bound vars, open_ty T U replaces index 0 by U and leaves others; under an inner All, indices shift. Then open_ty T' V replaces the inner index 0. So yes, all bound vars get eventually replaced by the types we quantify over, and no BVar remains at the leaves after enough openings — provided the original type is "well-formed" with respect to binding depth. But R is a Fixpoint on T that recurses into `open_ty T1 U`, which is not a syntactic subterm — this is not structurally recursive! Coq will reject a plain Fixpoint. We need a different approach: define R by recursion on the *structure* of T with a separate handling of the All case via a parameter/environment for bound variables.

Standard approach: define the relation with a *bound variable environment* (a list of semantic types/sets corresponding to enclosing binders, in de Bruijn levels). Since the type syntax uses de Bruijn indices for bound vars, at a Ty_All we push a new semantic value; at Ty_BVar i, look up. But the issue is that the type `Ty_All T` may be opened at arbitrary syntactic U later; the standard approach defines the relation on *open* types with free type variables (atoms) and uses the "open with fresh atom + environment" trick, and handles type instantiation via a substitution lemma, or uses a direct definition where All quantifies over syntactic types and uses substitution.

Let me think about how the standard locally nameless LR is done. Standard binary parametricity (Reynolds): 
- A type environment ρ maps each type variable X to a triple (U1, U2, R) where U1, U2 are types and R ⊆ Term × Term. Actually for the fundamental theorem one usually defines: given Delta, and two substitutions σ1, σ2 : Delta → Ty, and a relational substitution ρ : ΠX∈Delta. Rel(σ1 X, σ2 X) with ρ_X ⊆ ⟦σ1 X⟧ × ⟦σ2 X⟧ (i.e., related pairs are in the logical relations).
- Define ⟦T⟧_ρ ⊆ Term × Term for T with free vars in Delta.
- Ty_FVar X: ρ_X.
- Ty_Arrow T1 T2: pairs (f1,f2) such that for all (a1,a2) ∈ ⟦T1⟧_ρ, (f1 a1, f2 a2) ∈ ⟦T2⟧_ρ.
- Ty_All T: pairs (t1,t2) such that for all U1, U2, and all R ⊆ ⟦U1⟧ × ⟦U2⟧ (a relation between terms respecting the logical relations), and all fresh X, (t1 U1, t2 U2) ∈ ⟦T⟧_{ρ[X ↦ (U1,U2,R)]}... where ⟦T⟧ uses open_ty T X with X a free atom. Hmm, the type-level substitution again.

Alternatively, define ⟦T⟧ for *open* types directly with an environment that includes both type-level bound var interpretations and free var interpretations. Let me consider de Bruijn *levels* instead of indices: define the relation on types represented as (ty, context Delta) where Delta is the list of free type variable atoms; when we go under a binder, we can either (a) add the binder's semantic interpretation as a level, or (b) open the type with a fresh atom and add it to Delta.

Approach (a): Interpret types with an environment `env : list sem_ty` (de Bruijn *levels*: head = most recently bound). The relation function:

```
Fixpoint R (rho : atom -> semrel) (levels : list semrel) (T : ty) : tm -> tm -> Prop
```
But T still has de Bruijn *indices*, while levels is a stack indexed... At `Ty_BVar i`, look up the i-th element of `levels` (level for the innermost binder = head). At `Ty_All T`, we recurse with `S :: levels` for arbitrary S. This is structurally recursive on T! At `Ty_FVar X`, use rho X. This works and avoids type substitution at the binder level. But the typing judgment's All rule uses opening with a fresh atom. We need a bridge between `Ty_BVar` interpretation via levels and the typing rule's opening. The step behavior: `tapp (tabs t) U --> open_tm_ty t U`, and the LR should include a "step" closure to transfer relatedness from `open_tm_ty t U` to `tapp (tabs t) U`. So we need: for all U, `open_tm_ty t U ∈ ⟦T⟧_{levels with the new S = ⟦U⟧?}`. Hmm, but S is arbitrary. The environment S at the binder should be arbitrary, while opening at U gives the structural relation of U. If the All relation quantifies over arbitrary S, we need to prove `open_tm_ty t U` related for arbitrary S, which is not true in general (the relation at T depends on S and U's structure). Unless the All interpretation is defined differently: t ∈ ⟦∀X.T⟧ iff for all U and all arbitrary relations... but then the term is fixed and the type instantiation is at U.

OK here's the standard resolution: The fundamental theorem for T_TApp uses the All relation at syntactic U: from t ∈ ∀X.T conclude `tapp t U ∈ ⟦open_ty T U⟧`, where the All relation was defined as: "for all U and all relations R such that R is *contained in* ⟦U⟧ × ⟦U⟧ (or more generally sound for U), for all fresh X, open_tm_ty t X ∈ ⟦open_ty T X⟧_{X↦R}".

Then the T_TApp case concludes `tapp t U ∈ ⟦open_ty T U⟧`? But the All relation gives `open_tm_ty t X ∈ ...`, not `tapp t U`. Let's think about the substitution lemma in the binary relation setting:

Lemma (relational substitution): Suppose for all X in Delta, ρ(X) = (U1_X, U2_X, R_X) with R_X ⊆ ⟦U1_X⟧_{ρ0} × ⟦U2_X⟧_{ρ0}? Circular. Typically one sets up the theorem as: define the logical relation directly on types with a type environment ρ mapping variables to *relations on closed terms*, and prove: if Γ ⊢ t : T and environments... Actually many developments (e.g., the "Reynolds parametricity in Coq" formalizations) use a "type substitution" lemma: 

⟦T[U/X]⟧_{ρ} relates (s[U1/X], s[U2/X]) whenever s related at T under ρ[X ↦ ?]... 

Let me look for a cleaner formulation specifically suited to what we need. We need only the identity instance: t : ∀X.X→X and want (t U) v -->* v. Perhaps we can prove this *directly* by a specialized relation, avoiding the full generality of type substitution.

Specialized approach: Let's define a predicate on type-variable relations. We want to show: for any closed wf U, value v : U, closed t : ∀X.X→X, (t U) v -->* v.

Idea: Define "t is a parametric identity" as: for all U1, U2, R (where R is a relation on terms), and all a1, a2 with R a1 a2, we have R (t U1 a1) (t U2 a2) — hmm, but the relation R is arbitrary, and t U1 is not necessarily a value. But maybe define R to be the "reduction to v" relation, and use the fact that t is related to itself at ∀X.X→X via a logical relation with *type substitution handled by the LR's own definition*.

Alternative idea: use a *unary* logical relation where Ty_All is interpreted by quantifying over all *pairs* (U, R) where R is an arbitrary relation on terms, with the interpretation of the body at the free atom X mapped to R, and the term applied at U. To bridge, we need closure of the LR under type substitution: specifically, if s ∈ ⟦T[X]⟧_{X↦R}, then s[U/X] ∈ ⟦T[U]⟧_{X irrelevant}. This is false for arbitrary R (R must be related to ⟦U⟧). But in the final application, we choose R = {a | a -->* v}, and U arbitrary. The relation ⟦T[U]⟧ with T = X→X is ⟦U→U⟧: f ∈ ⟦U→U⟧ iff ∀a ∈ ⟦U⟧, f a ∈ ⟦U⟧. This is not the same as f ∈ R→R. So indeed we cannot go through the structural relation at U.

Wait wait. Let's reconsider what the All clause should say for the free theorem. In parametricity, `t : ∀X.X→X` implies for all U1, U2 and R ⊆ U1×U2, t U1 ~_R t U2 at X→X. Actually the relation is on terms; free term variables are opaque. The needed lemma: for all s1, s2, x, if (open_subst...)... Let me think in the standard locally nameless LR way.

Standard locally nameless LR (e.g., from Charguéraud's "The Locally Nameless Representation" and various) defines the relation on *open* terms: for a term `t` with bound vars, `open_tm t a` is the term opened with a value a. The LR for arrows is defined using opening with arbitrary related values. Specifically:

`(f1, f2) ∈ ⟦T1 → T2⟧` iff for all values v1, v2 related at T1, `(open_tm_body f1 v1, open_tm_body f2 v2) ∈ ⟦T2⟧`? But f1 is `tm_abs T1' t2` and opening the body with v is `open_tm t2 v`. So the relation at arrow directly quantifies over the *body* `t2` and the bound var. This means the LR isn't purely a relation on closed terms; it's defined on terms plus their binders. Alternatively, define the arrow LR on arbitrary terms f: `∀v1,v2 ∈ ⟦T1⟧, (app f v1, app f v2) ∈ ⟦T2⟧`, and prove the AppAbs compatibility using a substitution lemma: IH gives `open_tm t2 (fvar x)` related when `fvar x ∈ ⟦T1⟧`; substitution lemma says we can replace `fvar x` by related `v` in the term and preserve relatedness, given `open_tm t2 x ∈ ...`. Proving the substitution lemma for the LR is a standard induction on types + the term structure. But with the "app f v" style definition, the substitution lemma statement would be about relatedness of terms with free variables; the relation ⟦T⟧ is a predicate of pairs of terms with free variables allowed, and we need: if (s1[x], s2[x]) ∈ ⟦T⟧ and (a1,a2) ∈ ⟦T1⟧ then (s1[a1], s2[a2]) ∈ ⟦T⟧. Hmm, is this true? For T = T1→T2 with T1 = the type of x: s1 is a function term containing x. Substituting related terms for x should preserve relatedness if the relation is "parametric"... but ⟦T⟧ only constrains closed-ish behavior via arrows; the free var x appears in s1 as an opaque term and substituting a1 changes behavior. The relation at arrow asks: for all related args, app s1 arg related. Substituting x by a1 in s1: app (s1[a1]) arg = (app s1 arg)[a1]. So we'd need the lemma at T2 for app s1 arg, which by IH... this suggests proving a general substitution lemma by induction on the type, with the arrow case using the IH on T2 applied to the term `app s1 arg`, but that term depends on arg. Since the relation at T2 holds for all related args, we can apply IH at T2 to each instantiation. This is plausible: the substitution lemma "if (s1, s2) ∈ ⟦T⟧ then (s1[a1/x], s2[a2/x]) ∈ ⟦T⟧" for related a1,a2 *of the same type as x*. But what's "the type of x"? The relation at T doesn't know. Hmm, actually the statement would be for any related pair (a1,a2) at any type S: if (s1,s2) ∈ ⟦T⟧ then (subst x a1 s1, subst x a2 s2) ∈ ⟦T⟧. Is this true? For T an arrow: s1 related at T1→T2 means ∀ related args u1,u2 at T1, app s1 u1 ~ app s2 u2 at T2. After substituting x := a, we need app (s1[a1/x]) u1 ~ ...; note app (s1[a1/x]) u1 = (app s1 u1)[a1/x] syntactically (substitution commutes with app). So by IH at T2 (app s1 u1 related at T2), substitution preserves it. So the lemma holds for the arrow case if the relation is "closed under substitution of related terms for free variables" and this is proven by induction on T with the key fact that substitution commutes with the term constructors. For the All case: s1 related at ∀X.T means ∀ U1 U2 R, (tapp s1 U1, tapp s2 U2) ∈ ⟦open_ty T X⟧_{X↦R}... and substitution of term vars commutes with tm_tapp too. So the lemma seems provable by induction on the type structure, given the type-substitution machinery.

But wait — in the All case with the binary relation and type substitutions: what exactly is the semantic relation? Let's carefully define everything.

Let me set up a clean design.

### Semantic domain

Let `Rel := tm -> tm -> Prop`.

Type environment for free type variables: `tenv := atom -> Rel`? But we need soundness w.r.t. syntactic types to handle type substitution at TApp/T_Abs? Hmm, let me think about how T_TApp is handled.

Actually, let's revisit the fundamental theorem statement. Option 1: no syntactic type substitution at all; the terms stay as they are and the relation is on types with free atoms (the All case opens the body at a fresh atom and interprets that atom via an extended environment). Then:

`R : tenv -> ty -> tm -> tm -> Prop` (with tenv : atom -> Rel; the type ty has free vars interpreted by tenv; bound vars under Alls handled by opening).

But structural recursion on ty: for `Ty_All T`, we need to quantify over a fresh atom X, a relation S, and interpret `open_ty T (Ty_FVar X)` under tenv[X↦S]. The recursion is not on a subterm. Hmm, but we can define it with a *fuel/level* environment instead: define R with an extra list of relations for bound variables (de Bruijn levels). Let's do that:

```
Fixpoint R (rho : atom -> Rel) (lvl : list Rel) (T : ty) : tm -> tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => nth i lvl (fun _ _ => False)?
  | Ty_FVar X => rho X
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => fun t1 t2 => forall a1 a2, R rho lvl T1 a1 a2 -> R rho lvl T2 (tm_app t1 a1) (tm_app t2 a2)
  | Ty_All T1 => fun t1 t2 => forall S, R rho (S :: lvl) T1 (tm_tapp t1 ???) ...
  end
```

But at Ty_All, the terms t1, t2 are of type ∀X.T1, and the bound var in T1 refers to the All; we can't substitute a semantic relation into the term. Instead, the All relation should be: for all syntactic types U1, U2 and relations R' sound..., the applied terms `tapp t1 U1`, `tapp t2 U2` are related at T1 with the environment mapping the binder to... the structural relation of U1/U2? Or to R'?

Hmm, the cleanest is the classic Reynolds abstraction theorem with *type substitutions*:

**Definition.** A "relational type environment" for Delta consists of:
- two type substitutions σ1, σ2 : atom → ty,
- for each X ∈ Delta, a relation R_X : Rel with the soundness condition: for all terms a1 a2, R_X a1 a2 → ⟦σ1 X⟧ a1 a2-ish? Wait no — R_X should be a relation such that related terms are in the semantic types.

Actually, I recall the standard setup: ρ maps X to a triple (U1, U2, R) with `R ⊆ ⟦U1⟧_ρ0 × ⟦U2⟧_ρ0` where ρ0 is a base environment for... but U1, U2 might have free type variables too in general. To avoid nesting complexity, we could require σ1, σ2 to map into *closed* types and ρ_X to be arbitrary relations with no soundness requirement? But then how do we handle T_TApp? Let's see.

T_TApp case: t : ∀X.T, so has_type Delta Gamma t (Ty_All T), and the conclusion has_type Delta Gamma (tapp t U) (open_ty T U). Fundamental theorem conclusion: (tapp t σ1-part? ...). Hmm, with type substitutions, the term `tapp t U` gets substituted too: σ_i(tapp t U) = tm_tapp (σ_i t) (σ_i U). The IH for t : ∀X.T gives (σ1 t, σ2 t) ∈ ⟦∀X.T⟧_ρ. The All relation under ρ: for all U1, U2, and relation R', such that ???, (tapp (σ1 t) U1, tapp (σ2 t) U2) ∈ ⟦T⟧_{ρ[X ↦ (U1,U2,R')]}. We want to apply with U1 = σ1 U, U2 = σ2 U, R' = ???. The conclusion we need: (tapp (σ1 t) (σ1 U), tapp (σ2 t) (σ2 U)) ∈ ⟦open_ty T U⟧_ρ. But `open_ty T U` is a type in Delta (free vars from Delta), and its interpretation under ρ is a relation. Meanwhile the RHS from the All clause is ⟦T⟧_{ρ[X↦(U1,U2,R')]} where T is the *bound body* with de Bruijn indices. We need: ⟦open_ty T U⟧_ρ (as a relation on terms) equals/relates to ⟦T⟧_{ρ[X↦(...)]} where X's relation is R'. The natural choice: R' = the relation "a1 ~ a2 iff a1 ∈ ⟦open_ty T U⟧_ρ at the X slot..." — this is exactly a substitution lemma: interpret the bound variable X as the syntactic type U's interpretation. Let me think about a unary relation formulation that works:

Define `R (rho : atom -> tm -> Prop) (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop` where types with bound variables are handled by... Let's use the standard approach where `Ty_All T` is interpreted by opening at a *fresh free atom* X and quantifying over the predicate for X:

`R rho (Ty_All T) t := forall X S, ~In X (fv_ty ...) -> R (rho[X↦S]) (open_ty T (Ty_FVar X)) (tm_tapp t (Ty_FVar X))`

That is, the instantiation type is the fresh atom itself, treated as a free type variable. And then the term is `tapp t X` (a term with a free type variable X). The fundamental theorem then works nicely with T_TAbs: premise gives has_type (X::Delta) ... (open_tm_ty s X) (open_ty T X); IH with rho[X↦S] gives R rho[X↦S] (open_ty T X) (open_tm_ty s X). Hmm, so unary with fresh atoms also needs a type-substitution/relation-substitution bridge. The bridge in unary form: Let `R rho T t` with rho mapping free atoms to predicates. OK so here's the crux: To exploit parametricity, we need the *type variable* X to be interpreted by an arbitrary relation S, and we need to connect the abstract type-variable interpretation to the concrete instantiation at U. The connection must be: the term `tapp t U` and the term `tapp t X` (with X mapped to S) are related because... they're the same term with different type instantiations. **Standard Reynolds-style binary logical relation with type substitutions.**

Primitives:
- `Rel := tm -> tm -> Prop`.
- A type environment `TE` for a type context Δ consists of, for each X ∈ Δ, a triple (U1, U2, R) where U1, U2 : ty and R : Rel. Let me define the semantic relation on *open types* with respect to an environment ρ that maps free atoms to triples (U1, U2, R). Define:

⟦T⟧_ρ ⊆ tm × tm, for T whose free type variables are in dom(ρ).

- ⟦Ty_FVar X⟧_ρ := R_X.
- ⟦Ty_Arrow T1 T2⟧_ρ := { (f1,f2) | ∀(a1,a2) ∈ ⟦T1⟧_ρ. (app f1 a1, app f2 a2) ∈ ⟦T2⟧_ρ }.
- ⟦Ty_All T1⟧_ρ := { (t1,t2) | ∀ U1 U2 R, R ⊆ ⟦U1⟧_∅ × ⟦U2⟧_∅ → (tapp t1 U1, tapp t2 U2) ∈ ⟦T1⟧_{ρ[X↦(U1,U2,R)]} for fresh X }.
- ⟦Ty_BVar i⟧: none, because types are always opened at binders before reaching BVars? But in the All clause the body T1 is interpreted under ρ extended with X, but T1 itself contains Ty_BVar 0 for the binder... So the All clause must open T1 at a fresh atom X: ⟦open_ty T1 (Ty_FVar X)⟧_{ρ[X↦(U1,U2,R)]}. Then no BVar remains (assuming the type is well-formed and closed at the top, with all binders opened in this manner). Hmm, but this definition is not structurally recursive. Two issues: (1) recursion into open_ty; (2) the All clause mentions ⟦U1⟧_∅ to state soundness, and ⟦T1⟧ under the extended env.

To make it structurally recursive, use the level-environment approach: instead of opening the body at a fresh atom, interpret the body with an environment of relations for the bound variables (de Bruijn *indices* actually: the level stack). So:

⟦T⟧_{ρ, ξ} where ξ : list Rel is the stack for enclosing type binders (head = innermost binder).
- Ty_BVar i ↦ nth i ξ (with a default).
- Ty_FVar X ↦ ρ X.
- Ty_Arrow ↦ as above.
- Ty_All T1 ↦ { (t1,t2) | ∀ U1 U2 R, R ⊆ ⟦U1⟧_{∅,[]} × ⟦U2⟧_{∅,[]} → (tapp t1 U1, tapp t2 U2) ∈ ⟦T1⟧_{ρ, R :: ξ} }.

Wait, but now the soundness condition R ⊆ ⟦U1⟧ × ⟦U2⟧ is giving R as a "smaller" relation than the structural relation of U. And in the extended env, the binder is interpreted as R. Alternative standard approach (used in many Coq formalizations, e.g., "parametricity for System F" by Sozeau et al., or the logical relation in the "free theorems" literature): The fundamental theorem is stated for *closed* instances only via a *closing substitution*, and the relation at a type with free variables is indexed by an environment of relations on *closed types*: ρ(X) = (U1, U2, R) where U1,U2 are closed types and R ⊆ ⟦U1⟧ × ⟦U2⟧. And the soundness condition R' ⊆ ⟦U1⟧_∅ × ⟦U2⟧_∅: hmm, here U1 = σ1 U and U2 = σ2 U are syntactic types (in the top-level case closed); ⟦U1⟧_∅ and ⟦U2⟧_∅ are their structural relations with empty env. That's a "soundness of the relational interpretation" lemma: any pair related at U under ρ is also in the structural relations of the substituted types. This requires R_X ⊆ ⟦σ1 X⟧ × ⟦σ2 X⟧ for each free var X — exactly the admissibility condition for ρ! So the fundamental theorem's admissibility invariant is: each ρ(X) = (U1_X, U2_X, R_X) with R_X ⊆ ⟦U1_X⟧_∅ × ⟦U2_X⟧_∅. Then we can prove:

**Lemma (soundness / type substitution):** If ρ is admissible (each R_X ⊆ ⟦σ1 X⟧ × ⟦σ2 X⟧), then for every type T with free vars in dom ρ, ⟦T⟧_ρ ⊆ ⟦σ1 T⟧_∅ × ⟦σ2 T⟧_∅. 

Wait, but there's a subtlety: if T contains bound variables under Alls, the σ's are type substitutions and need to substitute inside the binder — but locally nameless de Bruijn indices: substitution of free atoms for Ty_FVar doesn't touch BVars; fine. However, in the All clause, the binder relation R' is a *new* free atom X's relation, not from ρ. When we compute ⟦T⟧_ρ for T = ∀Y.T', the All clause quantifies over U1, U2, R' admissible w.r.t. U1, U2, and interprets T' with binder ↦ R'. The soundness lemma for the outer type would say: if (t1,t2) ∈ ⟦∀Y.T'⟧_ρ, then (t1,t2) ∈ ⟦σ1(∀Y.T')⟧_∅ × ⟦σ2(...)⟧_∅ = ⟦∀Y.σ1 T'⟧_∅ × ... Admissibility: for each X ∈ dom ρ, ρ(X) = (U1_X, U2_X, R_X) with R_X ⊆ ⟦U1_X⟧_{∅,[]} × ⟦U2_X⟧_{∅,[]}. For each entry R in ξ... hmm, the level environment also needs associated types? That IS structurally recursive:

```
Fixpoint R (rho : atom -> Rel) (xi : list Rel) (T : ty) : tm -> tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => fun a b => exists? nth...
  | Ty_FVar X => rho X
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => fun a b => forall a1 a2, R rho xi T1 a1 a2 -> R rho xi T2 (tm_app a a1) (tm_app b a2)
  | Ty_All T1 => fun a b => forall U1 U2 R0, admissible U1 U2 R0 -> R rho (R0 :: xi) T1 (tm_tapp a U1) (tm_tapp b U2)
  end
```

Let me reformulate more concretely with the "two substitutions" baked into the environment. Let me think about what the All clause needs to produce for the term-level TApp rule:

Let me look at this from the literature: The standard "free theorem" proof for `forall a. a -> a` uses a logical relation where type variables are interpreted as arbitrary relations. The formalization usually proceeds:

1. Define `R : ty -> (ty -> ty -> Prop) env -> tm -> tm -> Prop` by recursion on ty, where the All case quantifies over types U1, U2 and relations.
2. Prove the "fundamental theorem"/abstraction theorem by induction on typing derivations, where the environment is a *type substitution* σ plus a relational environment.
3. Type substitution issues are handled by the fact that the relation at a type T under a relational env ρ with each ρ(X) ⊆ ⟦σ1 X⟧ × ⟦σ2 X⟧ is *contained in* ⟦σ1 T⟧ × ⟦σ2 T⟧ (the "soundness" lemma).

OK so with the level-environment approach, at Ty_All T1, the binder of the All is the de Bruijn index 0 in T1, and the semantic relation at that binder should be the relation assigned to the instantiation type. When the All is instantiated at syntactic U, the relation is ⟦U⟧_{ambient env}. In the level approach, `R rho (R0 :: xi) T1` where R0 = ⟦U⟧_ρ,ξ. Since open_ty T1 U replaces bound index 0 with U, and under inner binders the indices shift, and the inner binders are handled via levels... Let's verify: For T1 with a nested All, open_ty (Ty_All T2) U = Ty_All (open_ty_rec 1 U T2), which replaces index 1 with U inside T2 (under the inner All, the outer binder is index 1; the inner is index 0). The semantic interpretation with levels: ⟦Ty_All T2⟧ with level R0 :: xi says ∀V1 V2 R1, R1 adm → ⟦T2⟧ with levels R1 :: R0 :: xi. And ⟦open_ty_rec 1 U T2⟧ with levels R1 :: xi: does that equal ⟦T2⟧ with levels R1 :: R0 :: xi where R0 = ⟦U⟧_{xi}? The indices: inside T2, index 0 = inner binder ↦ R1 (head), index 1 = outer binder ↦ R0 (second), which matches: in open_ty_rec 1 U T2, the index 1 occurrences are replaced by U, interpreted as ⟦U⟧_{xi}; and index 0 stays as the inner binder, interpreted by R1 at the head of the level list. ✓. So the substitution lemma is straightforward: 

**Lemma (open_ty_rel):** For all T, U, rho, xi, and R0 := ⟦U⟧_{ρ,ξ}: ⟦open_ty T U⟧_{ρ,ξ} relates (a1,a2) iff ⟦T⟧_{ρ, R0::ξ} relates (a1,a2).

Wait, careful about the shifting: `open_ty_rec 0 U T` under binders increments k, so it replaces index k where k counts inner binders. In the level formulation, when we descend under k inner binders, the level list has k extra entries at the front, and the index k refers to the element at position k in the *current* level list = R0. So yes, the correspondence is: interpret open_ty T U under ξ iff interpret T under (⟦U⟧_ξ :: ξ). This should be provable by induction on T, for every ξ, T, U. Nice and clean.

But wait, there's a subtlety: this lemma requires ⟦U⟧_{ρ,ξ} to be a well-defined relation; and in the All clause we quantify over R0 with an admissibility condition, not necessarily of the form ⟦U⟧. For the TApp case we choose R0 = ⟦U⟧_{ρ,ξ}; admissibility needs to be proven: ⟦U⟧_{ρ,ξ} is admissible relative to the instantiation types... What exactly is admissibility? Let's define it to make both uses work:

Admissibility of R0 for a binder: R0 should be a relation such that related terms are "semantically of the two instantiation types". In the level framework, the binder doesn't have syntactic instantiation types unless we track them. Hmm. Let's think about why we need admissibility at all:

1. For the TApp case: the LR's All clause should give relatedness at ⟦open_ty T U⟧, and by the open_ty_rel lemma, we need ⟦T⟧ with the binder relation ⟦U⟧. So the All clause must allow R0 = ⟦U⟧_{ρ,ξ}. If admissibility is a restriction, ⟦U⟧_{ρ,ξ} must satisfy it.

2. For the final theorem: we need the All clause at relation R0 = {a | a -->* v} (unary treated as binary). Admissibility must be satisfied, which requires v ∈ ⟦U⟧ and backward closure.

But here's the key: what is the admissibility condition exactly? It should be: R0 ⊆ ⟦U1⟧ × ⟦U2⟧ where U1, U2 are the two syntactic instantiation types. In the level framework with a single syntactic type U shared? In the binary setup with two substitutions, the instantiation types are σ1 U and σ2 U. In the LR's All clause, we'd quantify over U1, U2 (syntactic types in the ambient Σ context) and R0 with R0 ⊆ ⟦U1⟧_{ρ,ξ} × ⟦U2⟧_{ρ,ξ}?? But ⟦U1⟧_{ρ,ξ} is a binary relation too...

Let me look at the *unary* version more carefully; maybe it's simpler and sufficient, and the apparent substitution problem resolves because with a unary relation, "R ⊆ ⟦U⟧" makes sense (predicate inclusion), and the open_ty_rel lemma with R0 = ⟦U⟧ gives the TApp case; for the final application we need R0 = S (the arbitrary predicate) included in ⟦U⟧, which holds if v ∈ ⟦U⟧ and backward closure. This seems very clean! Let's develop the unary version.

**Unary setup.**

- `Pred := tm -> Prop`.
- Environment `rho : atom -> Pred` for free type variables.
- Level environment `xi : list Pred` for bound type variables (de Bruijn indices).
- Definition (structural recursion on T):

```
Fixpoint R (rho : atom -> Pred) (xi : list Pred) (T : ty) : tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => nth i xi (fun _ => False)   (* note: nth with default *)
  | Ty_FVar X => rho X
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => fun t => forall a, R rho xi T1 a -> R rho xi T2 (tm_app t a)
  | Ty_All T1 => fun t => forall U (S : Pred), admissible rho xi U S -> R rho (S :: xi) T1 (tm_tapp t U)
  end.
```

Wait, but now the All clause uses `tapp t U` and the body T1 with binder ↦ S where S is admissible w.r.t. U: S ⊆ ⟦U⟧_{ρ,ξ}. Then open_ty_rel with R0 = ⟦U⟧ gives the TApp case... but hold on, the TApp case conclusion is at type `open_ty T U` and the LR's All clause gives relatedness at ⟦T1⟧_{S::ξ} for admissible S. With S = ⟦U⟧_{ρ,ξ}, the open_ty_rel lemma gives ⟦T1⟧_{⟦U⟧::ξ} = ⟦open_ty T1 U⟧_ξ. ✓. So the TApp case works if ⟦U⟧_{ρ,ξ} is admissible w.r.t. U, i.e., ⟦U⟧ ⊆ ⟦U⟧ trivially. ✓ (using the same ambient env).

With U = Ty_FVar X and ρ' = ρ[X↦S], ⟦Ty_FVar X⟧_{ρ',ξ} = rho'(X) = S. So ⟦open_ty T1 X⟧_{ρ[X↦S],ξ} = ⟦T1⟧_{ρ[X↦S], S::ξ}. Hmm, but the goal after opening the term is `open_tm_ty s U ∈ ⟦T1⟧_{S::ξ}` with atom env ρ (not extended with X). The type T1 is the body of the All, whose free atoms are from Δ; X is fresh so X ∉ T1. So rho[X↦S] and rho agree on T1's free atoms. Thus ⟦T1⟧_{ρ[X↦S], S::ξ} = ⟦T1⟧_{ρ, S::ξ}. ✓.

And the term `open_tm_ty s X` vs `open_tm_ty s U`: these are different! The IH gives relatedness of `open_tm_ty s X` with X as an atom, but we need `open_tm_ty s U` with U a syntactic type. So we need a **type instantiation lemma**: if a term (with free type variables) is related at a type where the free atom X is interpreted as S, then substituting a syntactic type U for X... Something like:

**Lemma (term type-substitution)**: If X is a free atom, U a type, ρ an atom env, ξ a level env, and S := ⟦U⟧_{ρ,ξ} (or S ⊆ ⟦U⟧), then: for any term s and any predicate... hmm.

Let's think about what terms are involved: s is the body of a type abstraction. We know: `open_tm_ty s X ∈ ⟦open_ty T1 X⟧_{ρ[X↦S], ξ}` for all S (well, from IH with S). We need to relate s[U/X] to s[X/X]=s? The term `open_tm_ty s U` vs `open_tm_ty s X`: both are s with the *type* instantiated differently. There's no term-level structure connecting them other than type substitution.

Could we define the LR so that it is *closed under type substitution by any type whose interpretation is included in the variable's predicate*? I.e., a lemma:

**Lemma (ty_subst_lr)**: If s ∈ ⟦T⟧_{ρ[X↦S], ξ}, and S' ⊆ S (predicate inclusion), hmm no...

Let's think about what `open_tm_ty s X ∈ ⟦open_ty T1 X⟧_{ρ[X↦S]}` gives for the term `open_tm_ty s U`: The type `open_ty T1 U` is structurally the same except the variable is U rather than X. The relation ⟦open_ty T1 U⟧_{ρ,ξ} is computed structurally from U's structure. The term s[U/X] is the same term as s[X/X] with types replaced. Intuitively, the term's behavior is unaffected by type erasure (the semantics ignores types except for annotations, and the annotation types don't affect reduction!). Indeed, look at the step relation: types in annotations are irrelevant to reduction, except that typing rules require locally_closed_ty T for tapp steps. The term's reduction only depends on the term structure, not on the type annotations. And the logical relation at a type T depends on T (via the structural definition), so different annotation types could matter for relatedness. But perhaps we can prove a general lemma: **relatedness is invariant under type substitution in the term and the type, as long as the substituted type's interpretation refines the variable's predicate**. Let's formalize:

Claim: Let ρ' = ρ[X↦S] where S ⊆ ⟦U⟧_{ρ,ξ} (S is *contained in* the interpretation of U). Then for any type T with X free and term s:
   if s ∈ ⟦T⟧_{ρ',ξ} then s[U/X] ∈ ⟦T[U/X]⟧_{ρ,ξ}?
Hmm, is this true? Check T = X, s ∈ S (i.e., s ∈ S). Conclusion: s[U/X] = s ∈ ⟦U⟧_{ρ,ξ}. This requires S ⊆ ⟦U⟧. ✓ (that's the assumption). Check T = Arrow T1 T2: s ∈ ⟦T1→T2⟧_{ρ'} means ∀a, a ∈ ⟦T1⟧_{ρ'} → s a ∈ ⟦T2⟧_{ρ'}. Need: ∀a, a ∈ ⟦T1[U/X]⟧_ρ → s[U/X] a ∈ ⟦T2[U/X]⟧_ρ. Hmm, we need to relate a ∈ ⟦T1[U/X]⟧_ρ with a ∈ ⟦T1⟧_{ρ'}: if the induction hypothesis gives ⟦T1[U/X]⟧_ρ =? ⟦T1⟧_{ρ'} under S ⊆ ⟦U⟧ — is that an equality? For T1 = Y (another free var), ⟦Y⟧_ρ vs ⟦Y⟧_{ρ'}: both ρ(Y). Fine. For T1 = X: ⟦U⟧_ρ vs S. These are not equal, only S ⊆ ⟦U⟧. Hmm. Let's check the arrow case concretely: Claim: if S ⊆ ⟦U⟧_ρ, then for any T, ⟦T[U/X]⟧_ρ ⊆ ⟦T⟧_{ρ[X↦S]}? [i.e., concrete ⊆ abstract]. Check T = X: ⟦U⟧_ρ ⊆ S? False (S ⊆ ⟦U⟧ is the wrong direction). Other direction: ⟦T⟧_{ρ[X↦S]} ⊆ ⟦T[U/X]⟧_ρ? Check T = X: S ⊆ ⟦U⟧ ✓ by assumption. Check T = T1→T2: Suppose f ∈ ⟦T1→T2⟧_{ρ[X↦S]}: ∀a ∈ ⟦T1⟧_{ρ[X↦S]}, f a ∈ ⟦T2⟧_{ρ[X↦S]}. Want: ∀a ∈ ⟦T1[U/X]⟧_ρ, f a ∈ ⟦T2[U/X]⟧_ρ. By IH (for T2): ⟦T2⟧_{ρ[X↦S]} ⊆ ⟦T2[U/X]⟧_ρ, so f a ∈ ⟦T2[U/X]⟧. But we need a ∈ ⟦T1⟧_{ρ[X↦S]}, and we only have a ∈ ⟦T1[U/X]⟧_ρ. The IH for T1 gives the wrong direction (it would give: ⟦T1⟧_{ρ[X↦S]} ⊆ ⟦T1[U/X]⟧). So this doesn't work. Right — this is the classic issue: the logical relation at type variable X with relation S is not comparable to the structural relation at U unless S = ⟦U⟧ exactly (and even then, equality holds by induction). 

So the right approach is to set S := ⟦U⟧ exactly (equality), so that the type substitution lemma becomes an *equality*: ⟦T[U/X]⟧_ρ = ⟦T⟧_{ρ[X↦⟦U⟧_ρ]} (when suitable). Then for the final free theorem, we need the relation at the top-level All instance to be the arbitrary R = {a | a -->* v}, not necessarily equal to ⟦U⟧. But note: in the final theorem, we apply the All clause at U with relation S — the LR's All clause quantifies over ALL predicates S (with admissibility? maybe no restriction!), and gives `tapp t U ∈ ⟦T1⟧_{S::ξ}`. For the term `tapp t U`, the All clause works *directly* and doesn't need any type substitution! The type substitution lemma is only needed for the T_TApp case in the fundamental lemma, where we need to relate the goal type `open_ty T1 U` with the LR body relation. And there we can choose S = ⟦U⟧.

Wait, so then the All clause quantifies over arbitrary S (no admissibility needed!), and:

- Final theorem: choose S = {a | a -->* v}. ✓.
- T_TAbs case: goal ∀U S, `tapp (tabs s) U ∈ ⟦T1⟧_{S::ξ}`. By backward closure, suffices `open_tm_ty s U ∈ ⟦T1⟧_{S::ξ}`. By open_ty_rel (with U = Ty_FVar X): `⟦open_ty T1 X⟧_{ρ[X↦S],ξ} = ⟦T1⟧_{ρ[X↦S], S::ξ}`. And since X fresh doesn't occur free in T1, ⟦T1⟧_{ρ[X↦S],S::ξ} = ⟦T1⟧_{ρ,S::ξ}. But the term we need is `open_tm_ty s U`, not `open_tm_ty s X`! Ah, here's the crux again: the TAbs premise is about opening with a fresh atom X, but the operational step opens with the syntactic type U.

Hmm!! So in the T_TAbs case, the term-level instantiation differs: IH gives s opened at X, goal needs s opened at U. So we need a lemma: relatedness of `open_tm_ty s X` at ⟦open_ty T1 X⟧_{ρ[X↦S]} implies relatedness of `open_tm_ty s U` at ⟦open_ty T1 U⟧_{ρ} for arbitrary U with S... Wait, but this is exactly the type substitution lemma with equality issues. Hmm, but here S is arbitrary (the goal's S). So we'd need: for all types U, predicates S, if s[X] ∈ ⟦T1[X]⟧_{X↦S} then s[U] ∈ ⟦T1[U]⟧_{?}... with what predicate? The goal is ⟦T1⟧_{S::ξ} after open_ty_rel with S at the binder, i.e., ⟦open_ty T1 U⟧_{ρ,ξ} = ⟦T1⟧_{ρ,⟦U⟧::ξ}. The IH is at ⟦T1⟧_{ρ[X↦S], S::ξ}. Since S ≠ ⟦U⟧ in general, the relations differ. So type substitution with an arbitrary relation at X cannot give the structural interpretation at U. This is the fundamental obstacle.

How do standard proofs handle this? Let me recall the actual structure of Reynolds' abstraction theorem. The key: the relation at a *type abstraction* is defined as: for all U1, U2, R admissible, (t U1, t U2) ∈ ⟦T⟧ with X ↦ R. The typing rule T_TAbs premise gives (s X, s X) related at T[X] with X↦R for a fresh X, for all R. Then to conclude (t U1, t U2) related, we use the fact that `t U1 --> s[U1/X]` and the substitution lemma: **if s[X] is related at T[X] with X↦R, then s[U1/X] and s[U2/X] are related at T[X] with X↦R**, where R is the relation at the binder — and crucially, this substitution lemma is a *term-level substitution* where the substituted types U1, U2 come with the relation R being *between* their interpretations: R relates terms of type U1 and U2. The lemma is not about the structural interpretation of U1/U2; it's about substituting U1 and U2 into the term while keeping the same relation environment R. So the type T after substitution is T[U1/X] on one side and T[U2/X] on the other — two different syntactic types! The LR statement in the binary case is ⟦T⟧_ρ which is defined on a single syntactic type T, and the two terms have types σ1 T, σ2 T. The environment ρ then encodes the substitutions. So:

- The binary relation ⟦T⟧_ρ is on a single syntactic type T; ρ maps each free atom X to a triple (U1_X, U2_X, R_X) where R_X relates terms of types U1_X, U2_X.
- Admissibility: R_X ⊆ ⟦U1_X⟧_{∅} × ⟦U2_X⟧_{∅} (both closed under empty env) — needed for the All case's structural reading and the final application... but actually maybe admissibility is needed to prove the "value/typing soundness" or the substitution lemma.

In the T_TAbs case with the binary setup: goal: (tabs s, tabs s) ∈ ⟦Ty_All T1⟧_ρ (assuming closed type?), i.e., ∀ U1 U2 R, adm → (tapp (tabs s) U1, tapp (tabs s) U2) ∈ ⟦T1⟧_{ρ[X↦(U1,U2,R)]}. By backward closure, suffices (open_tm_ty s U1, open_tm_ty s U2) ∈ ⟦T1⟧_{ρ[X↦(U1,U2,R)]}. The IH gives: for all fresh Y (a fresh *atom*), (open_tm_ty s Y, open_tm_ty s Y) ∈ ⟦open_ty T1 Y⟧_{ρ'[Y↦(U1,U2,R)]}? Hmm, wait: the IH is for the typing premise under type context Δ∪{Y}, with the type `open_ty T1 Y` and the term `open_tm_ty s Y`. The fundamental theorem's type environment for Δ∪{Y} must map Y to some triple; applying with Y ↦ (U1,U2,R) gives (σ1(open_tm_ty s Y), σ2(open_tm_ty s Y)) ∈ ⟦open_ty T1 Y⟧_{ρ[Y↦(U1,U2,R)]}. Now σ1 maps Y ↦ U1 and other atoms via ρ; so σ1(open_tm_ty s Y) = open_tm_ty (σ1 s) U1. Similarly σ2 → open_tm_ty (σ2 s) U2. And the goal is (open_tm_ty (σ1 s) U1, open_tm_ty (σ2 s) U2) ∈ ⟦T1⟧_{ρ[X↦...]} where X is the binder's atom... Hmm, careful: the binder in `Ty_All T1` is de Bruijn, so T1 has Ty_BVar 0. In the level framework, ⟦T1⟧_{ρ, R::ξ}. In the atom framework, open T1 at Y. Using open_ty_rel (level formulation), we get ⟦open_ty T1 Y⟧_{ρ[Y↦...],ξ} = ⟦T1⟧_{ρ[Y↦...], R::ξ}? Wait, but with the atom Y interpreted via the *atom env* as (U1,U2,R) and the level approach... Let me redo this in the binary level framework.

I'll set up the binary level framework carefully:

Environment: `ρ : atom -> entry`, where `entry := {U1 : ty; U2 : ty; R : Rel}`. Level env: `ξ : list Rel` (for bound type variables). But we need the *types* associated with bound variables too? In the All clause, when we instantiate at syntactic U1, U2, we get a relation R admissible relative to U1, U2; we push R onto ξ. The type-level body T1 has Ty_BVar 0 referring to R. When later we open T1 at a syntactic type (via open_ty_rel), the binder relation is ⟦U⟧... For the substitution lemma we need to know that pushing R onto ξ corresponds to substituting the types U1, U2. Since R is just a relation, and the body T1's BVar 0 is interpreted as R, that's consistent regardless of U1, U2. The connection to the term-level substitution (open_tm_ty with U1/U2) is handled by the IH: the IH gives relatedness of terms opened at the *atom* Y (not at U1/U2)! Hmm, in the fundamental theorem, the term being typed in the T_TAbs premise is `open_tm_ty s Y`, and the substitution σ1 maps Y to U1, so the term at the conclusion is `σ1(open_tm_ty s Y) = open_tm_ty (σ1 s) U1`. So the type substitution happens through σ1, which substitutes *free atoms* Y in the term. That's exactly why the fundamental theorem uses explicit type substitutions σ1, σ2! The IH is applied with an environment where Y ↦ (U1, U2, R), so σ1(Y) = U1, σ2(Y) = U2. Then the IH conclusion directly gives the terms opened at U1 and U2. 

So the fundamental theorem's statement includes the type substitutions σ1, σ2 extracted from the environment: for each atom X in Δ, entry(X) = (U1_X, U2_X, R_X), and σ1 = fun X => U1_X, σ2 = fun X => U2_X. The theorem: if has_type Δ Γ t T, then for all admissible ρ (and term env σ), (σ1(t), σ2(t)) ∈ ⟦T⟧_{ρ,ξ}... But wait, in the goal, the relation ⟦T⟧_{ρ,ξ} is computed structurally on T, with free atoms interpreted via ρ's relations. The two terms σ1(t), σ2(t) have types σ1(T), σ2(T). Admissibility of each entry: R_X ⊆ ⟦U1_X⟧_∅ × ⟦U2_X⟧_∅ (with closed types, empty env). 

Now the T_TApp case: has_type Δ Γ t (Ty_All T1) and has_type Δ Γ (tapp t U) (open_ty T1 U). IH: (σ1 t, σ2 t) ∈ ⟦Ty_All T1⟧_{ρ,ξ}. The All clause (as defined on the level framework) gives: ∀U1, U2, R admissible → (tapp (σ1 t) U1, tapp (σ2 t) U2) ∈ ⟦T1⟧_{ρ, R::ξ}. We need the goal: (σ1(tapp t U), σ2(tapp t U)) = (tapp (σ1 t) (σ1 U), tapp (σ2 t) (σ2 U)) ∈ ⟦open_ty T1 U⟧_{ρ,ξ}. Choose U1 = σ1 U, U2 = σ2 U, R := ⟦U⟧_{ρ,ξ}. Need admissibility: ⟦U⟧_{ρ,ξ} ⊆ ⟦σ1 U⟧_∅ × ⟦σ2 U⟧_∅ — the soundness lemma. And need: ⟦open_ty T1 U⟧_{ρ,ξ} ⊇/relates same as ⟦T1⟧_{ρ, ⟦U⟧_{ρ,ξ}::ξ} — the open_ty_rel lemma with R := ⟦U⟧_{ρ,ξ}. ✓.

For the soundness/admissibility lemma, we need: for every type V in the ambient context, ⟦V⟧_{ρ,ξ} ⊆ ⟦σ1 V⟧_{∅,[]} × ⟦σ2 V⟧_{∅,[]}. This is proven by induction on V, using the admissibility of the entries for free atoms and of the level relations for bound vars. But the level relations R in ξ also need admissibility conditions relative to the types they instantiate! Hmm, the level env ξ doesn't track types. When we push R = ⟦U⟧_{ρ,ξ} onto ξ in the All clause... and then prove the soundness lemma for a type V under the extension, the bound var 0 is interpreted as R. Under what substitutions? There's a mismatch: σ1, σ2 for the ambient context are fixed; the bound var's "instantiation types" are σ1 U, σ2 U, but U is a type in the ambient context, and σ1 U is its closed instance.

Let me reformulate the soundness lemma including the level env. Define a notion of "admissible level env" ξ w.r.t. two substitutions σ1, σ2 and a type substitution for the bound variables? Actually, the types substituted for bound variables can differ per side. In the All clause, the binder is instantiated at U1 on the left and U2 on the right (two potentially different syntactic types). So the level env should be a list of *triples* (V1, V2, R) where V1, V2 are types in the ambient context (or closed), and R ⊆ ⟦V1⟧ × ⟦V2⟧... This is getting complicated, but maybe manageable if we treat the bound-variable level entries also as triples with types, and prove the soundness lemma by induction.

```
Definition rel := tm -> tm -> Prop.

Fixpoint interp (T : ty) (env : nat -> rel) : rel :=
  match T with
  | TyVar x => env x
  | Arr T1 T2 => fun e1 e2 => forall v1 v2, interp T1 env v1 v2 -> interp T2 env (App e1 v1) (App e2 v2)
  | All T1 => fun e1 e2 => forall (R : rel) (x : nat), interp T1 (extend env x R) (TApp e1 x) (TApp e2 x)
  end.
```

Here types are *locally nameless with atoms* for free vars and de Bruijn for bound? Let me now state the theorem more carefully in the unary setting:

**Environments.**
- Type substitution σ : atom → ty (closed types).
- Atom relational env `rho : atom -> pred` (only for atoms in Δ; but as a function total on atoms, assigning arbitrary predicates elsewhere).
- Level env `xi : list (ty * pred)` for bound type variables, innermost first.

Let's simplify the design: maybe we can avoid all the wf machinery by defining the LR on *all* types with a total environment, using lists/nth defaults. Let me think about what's truly needed for each typing rule's semantic obligation:

The fundamental theorem's semantic obligations:
1. T_Var: term fvar x, type T from Γ. Term env maps x to a *term* a with a ∈ ⟦T⟧ (semantic context). Conclusion: fvar x ∈ ⟦T⟧. So the term env must be such that fvar x itself is related, or the relation must be closed under substituting the free var? Hmm, in the fundamental theorem with free term variables, the standard approach: the semantic term env maps x to a term a_x ∈ ⟦Γ(x)⟧, and the theorem concludes the *substituted* term t[a_x/x] ∈ ⟦T⟧. For closed t (Γ empty), no substitution. Since our target has Γ=[] and the TAbs case introduces x in Γ, we need to handle term substitution. The TAbs case: IH gives `(open_tm t2 (fvar x))[a/x] = open_tm t2 a` ∈ ⟦T2⟧ for a ∈ ⟦T1⟧; conclusion: tm_abs T1 t2 ∈ ⟦T1→T2⟧, which requires ∀a ∈ ⟦T1⟧, tm_app (abs T1 t2) a ∈ ⟦T2⟧; by backward closure (app (abs ...) a --> open_tm t2 a) suffices open_tm t2 a ∈ ⟦T2⟧. So the semantic term env substitution is exactly what makes TAbs work! Great. So the fundamental theorem should substitute the term env: term variables x ∈ Γ get terms a_x (closed? or at least semantically related), and the theorem concludes t[a_x/x] ∈ ⟦T⟧. But the substitution is simultaneous for all x; since the syntax uses free atoms for term vars and de Bruijn for bound, `tm_subst` handles one variable at a time. We can define a simultaneous substitution function `tm_subst_all σ_term t` recursively, or work with a single-variable substitution lemma. Since in the TAbs case we open with fvar x and then substitute x ↦ a, we can use `tm_subst x a` (single). The IH gives relatedness of `open_tm t2 (fvar x)` under the semantic env extended with x ↦ a; the conclusion after substitution is `tm_subst x a (open_tm t2 (fvar x))`. Is that equal to `open_tm t2 a`? In locally nameless with capture-avoiding substitution: `open_tm t2 (fvar x)` substitutes de Bruijn 0 with fvar x; then substituting x with a gives open_tm t2 a. The two substitutions commute (substituting a free var and a bound var). This should be provable by induction on t2, but it's a tedious lemma. Alternatively, define the fundamental theorem with a *single* distinguished free variable and avoid the general substitution by using the fact that the semantic term env can be typed as a function and the theorem concludes `tm_subst_all`. Similar issue.

This suggests an approach via normalization: 
1. Prove strong normalization / that every typable term reduces to a value ("progress + preservation" or reducibility).
2. Analyze normal forms.

But step 1 (strong normalization for System F) requires reducibility candidates with the logical relation for termination, which is a similar amount of infrastructure to parametricity (but arguably simpler: the reducibility candidate interpretation has type variables mapped to arbitrary *sets of terminating terms*, and the All case quantifies over all candidates; same fundamental lemma structure!). - Typing implies local closure of terms and types?    Definition pred := tm -> Prop.
   Fixpoint R (rho : atom -> pred) (xi : list pred) (T : ty) : pred :=

     | Ty_BVar i => match nth_error xi i with Some P => P | None => fun _ => False end

     end.

In the fundamental theorem's T_App case: goal: `app (σ t1) (σ t2) ∈ ⟦T2⟧`. IH1: σ t1 ∈ ⟦T1→T2⟧ (semantic arrow: for all *values* a ∈ ⟦T1⟧, app (σ t1) a ∈ ⟦T2⟧). IH2: σ t2 ∈ ⟦T1⟧. To conclude, we need σ t2 to be a value! But t2 is an arbitrary term of type T1; its substituted term σt2 need not be a value. Hmm! So the arrow relation's restriction to values doesn't suffice for the T_App typing rule. Standard CBV logical relations handle this by defining the semantic type interpretation as: `t ∈ ⟦T⟧` iff `t` is a value in the "value interpretation" OR `t` reduces appropriately. I.e., the semantic interpretation is the *closure* of the value interpretation under reduction: `t ∈ ⟦T⟧` iff for all... Actually the standard "logical relation" for CBV (as in TAPL chapter on parametricity) defines, for each type T, a set of *values* V⟦T⟧, and a term t is in ⟦T⟧ iff t evaluates to a value in V⟦T⟧ (t ⇓ v ∧ v ∈ V⟦T⟧). Then:
- Arrow: f ∈ V⟦T1→T2⟧ iff λx.t = f and ∀v ∈ V⟦T1⟧, t[v/x] ∈ ⟦T2⟧.
- All: t ∈ V⟦∀X.T⟧ iff t = ΛX.s and ∀U, R, s[U/X] ∈ ⟦T⟧_{X↦R}.
And the fundamental theorem: Γ ⊢ t : T, env maps vars to values in V⟦Γ⟧, then t[env] ∈ ⟦T⟧ (evaluates to a related value).

In the T_App case: IH1 gives σt1 ∈ ⟦T1→T2⟧ meaning σt1 evaluates to a value f ∈ V⟦T1→T2⟧; IH2 gives σt2 ∈ ⟦T1⟧ meaning σt2 evaluates to a value a ∈ V⟦T1⟧. Then app (σt1)(σt2) evaluates to app f a (by congruence), then f a evaluates to the body applied to a, which is in ⟦T2⟧ by the value interpretation; so the whole thing is in ⟦T2⟧. This requires multi-step congruence lemmas (app steps through evaluation) and the "evaluation" definition. This is more infrastructure but standard.

Alternatively, define the relation with the "reduction closure" so that relatedness at every type is closed under reduction on both sides (forward and backward), and prove the T_App case by: `app (σt1)(σt2) ∈ ⟦T2⟧` if we can show it reduces to something related. With σt2 ∈ ⟦T1⟧, we need a lemma that related terms at T1 reduce (multi-step) to values related at T1. That's a "value lemma" / "fundamental property" of the relation: every related term has a reduction to a value (or is a value). And the arrow relation isn't defined on values only but on arbitrary terms with a semantic clause like: f ∈ ⟦T1→T2⟧ iff ∀a value related, f a ∈ ⟦T2⟧, AND f itself reduces to a value? Hmm.

The cleanest for CBV is the standard "logical relation with an evaluation semantics" (a.k.a. the "CIU" or "step-indexed" style). Let me think about what's minimal for our specific needs.

Now the type-variable case: R(Ty_FVar X)(t) = ∃v, t -->* v ∧ rho(X)(v). The fundamental theorem's conclusion at T=Ty_FVar X for the term fvar x is R(rho X)(a_x) ✓ from env. So we need multi-step congruence lemmas: app with value on left and multi-step on the right; app with multi-step on the left; tapp congruence; and the beta/tapp steps. And backward/forward closure of R under multi-step (easy from the definition).

This is the standard approach and seems clean. The benefit: the relations are all "reduction-closed" by construction, and the typing rules' congruence cases are handled by multi-step reasoning. The cost: proving multi-step congruence lemmas (multi_app_l, multi_app_r, multi_tapp, multi_trans, multi_choice?) and lc facts for the step rules.

Let's now carefully define everything.

**Definitions.**

```
Definition sem := tm -> Prop.  (* semantic type: predicate on terms *)

Fixpoint V (rho : atom -> sem) (xi : list sem) (T : ty) : tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => match nth_error xi i with Some P => P | None => fun _ => False end
  | Ty_FVar X => rho X
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => fun t => forall a, R rho xi T1 a -> R rho xi T2 (tm_app t a)
  | Ty_All T1 => fun t => forall U R0, lc_ty_at 0 U -> R rho (R0 :: xi) T1 (tm_tapp t U)
  end
with R (rho : atom -> sem) (xi : list sem) (T : ty) : tm -> Prop :=
  fun t => exists v, multi t v /\ V rho xi T v.
```

Hmm, mutual recursion: V refers to R (in the arrow case), and R refers to V. In Coq, a mutual Fixpoint over the same type argument should work: both V and R recurse on T (structurally decreasing); the arrow case of V calls R at T1 and T2 (subterms) — structurally smaller ✓; R calls V at the same T ✓ (not smaller, but mutual recursion R → V at same T is allowed if we use `with` and the recursion is on the same argument? Coq's guard checker: for mutual fixpoints, calls between them must be on structurally smaller arguments or the same? A call from R to V at the same T is a "call to a mutual function on the same argument" — the guard condition requires that recursive calls be on a strict subterm, unless the call is to another function in the same block with the same or smaller argument? Coq allows this: in mutual fixpoints, calls from one function to another on a *decreasing* argument are fine, and calls on the *same* argument are allowed only in some cases? I recall that mutual fixpoints allow `f (S n)` calling `g n`, but `g n` calling `f n` is not allowed because the recursive argument must decrease. Hmm, actually Coq's guard condition for mutual fixpoints requires each recursive call (to any function in the block) to be on a structurally smaller argument than the caller's principal argument. Calling V from R at the same T is not smaller, so it would be rejected.

Solution: define R in terms of V without mutual recursion:
```
Fixpoint V ... T : tm -> Prop := ...
Definable: R rho xi T t := exists v, multi t v /\ V rho xi T v.
```
Since V is a Fixpoint and R is a Definition using V, no mutual recursion. But V's arrow case refers to R: `fun t => forall a, R rho xi T1 a -> R rho xi T2 (app t a)`. R must be defined before V, but R depends on V. Circular again.

Options:
(a) Inline the reduction-closure in V's arrow case: `forall a, (exists v, multi a v /\ V rho xi T1 v) -> (exists v, multi (app t a) v /\ V rho xi T2 v)`. Then V is self-recursive; R remains a definition. ✓. Similarly, the All case: `forall U R0, lc_ty_at 0 U -> (exists v, multi (tapp t U) v ∧ V rho (R0::xi) T1 v)`. So V is defined with the "R" inlined as the exists-multi-V form. That's fine but verbose. We can define `R` as a local abbreviation? In Coq, within a Fixpoint, referring to a previously defined function is fine, but R needs V. We could define a *parameterized* "closure" combinator that doesn't mention V? No, the closure's body mentions V at subterms, which is the recursive call.

(b) Define V with the arrow case referencing only V (not R) by quantifying over values: `forall a, value a -> V rho xi T1 a -> R...` — but still needs R for the result. Hmm.

(c) Use `Fixpoint V (T) := match T with Arrow T1 T2 => fun t => forall a, (exists v, multi a v /\ V T1 v) -> (exists v, multi (app t a) v /\ V T2 v) ...` and define `R rho xi T t := exists v, multi t v /\ V rho xi T v` after. In the arrow body, the recursive calls are on T1 and T2 (smaller) ✓. So self-recursion works. Good. Let's do that.

Actually, we can make it cleaner by defining an auxiliary notation: since Coq Fixpoints can use local definitions inside the body? We can write the exists form directly.

Let's write:

```
Fixpoint V (rho : atom -> sem) (xi : list sem) (T : ty) : tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i =>
      match nth_error xi i with
      | Some P => P
      | None => fun _ => False
      end
  | Ty_FVar X => rho X
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      fun t =>
        forall a,
          (exists v, multi a v /\ V rho xi T1 v) ->
          (exists v, multi (tm_app t a) v /\ V rho xi T2 v)
  | Ty_All T1 =>
      fun t =>
        forall U (R0 : sem),
          lc_ty_at 0 U ->
          (exists v, multi (tm_tapp t U) v /\ V rho (R0 :: xi) T1 v)
  end.

Definition R rho xi T t := exists v, multi t v /\ V rho xi T v.
```

Wait, there's a subtlety: in the arrow case, I inline `R rho xi T1 a` as `exists v, multi a v /\ V rho xi T1 v`. The recursive calls V rho xi T1 v and V rho xi T2 v are on subterms ✓. In the All case, the recursive call V rho (R0::xi) T1 v is on the subterm T1 ✓. Structural recursion ✓.

Note: in the arrow case, we quantify over *arbitrary* a with R(T1) a, not just values. This is as discussed; the caller (T_App case) will need to supply σt2 ∈ R(T1) (arbitrary term). This is why standard CBV logical relations define the *value* interpretation separately, requiring values. The relation R(T) is then defined as "t reduces to a value in V(T)". And the value interpretation V(T) must be a predicate only on values. So:

```
V(T)(v) with an implicit requirement value v (or defined only for values).

Arrow: V(Arrow T1 T2)(abs T1' t2) := ∀a, R(T1) a → R(T2)(app (abs T1' t2) a) — for a ∈ R(T1), a reduces to a value a' with V(T1)(a'), so app (abs) a -->* app (abs) a' --> beta (value a' ✓) → open_tm t2 a' ∈ R(T2) by the clause for values a' ∈ V(T1). So the arrow value clause can be: ∀a, value a → V(T1) a → R(T2)(open? app (abs) a). Then the general a ∈ R(T1) is handled by reduction. Since app (abs) a -->* app (abs) a' and then beta, and R(T2) is closed under multi-step (if app(abs)a' ∈ R(T2) and app(abs)a -->* app(abs)a', then app(abs)a ∈ R(T2)) ✓.

So the final proof works, ASSUMING the fundamental theorem is proven. Let's now carefully design the fundamental theorem and all the lemmas.

**Fundamental theorem statement:**

```
Theorem fundamental :
  forall Delta Gamma t T,
    has_type Delta Gamma t T ->
    forall (sigma_ty : atom -> ty),
      (forall X, lc_ty_at 0 (sigma_ty X)) ->
    forall (rho : atom -> sem) (sigma_tm : atom -> tm),
      (forall x V, lookup_context x Gamma = Some V -> R rho [] V (sigma_tm x)) ->
      R rho [] T (subst_tm_ty sigma_ty sigma_tm t).
```

where `subst_tm_ty σ_ty σ_tm t := tm_subst_all σ_tm (tm_ty_subst_all σ_ty t)` (both simultaneous substitutions).

Hmm, we need to be careful about what "R rho [] T (substituted term)" means when T contains free type atoms in Δ: R interprets T's free atoms via rho. But there's no constraint tying rho to σ_ty! In the T_Var case, the env hypothesis directly gives R rho [] V (σ_tm x) for the variable's type V — but the substituted term's type is σ_ty V; the relation R rho [] V is on V with rho. Fine, no need to tie rho to σ_ty for the *relation* semantics. But wait, is the theorem true without any relation between rho and σ_ty? Consider T_App: we need to apply the arrow relation at type T1 to the argument σt2 ∈ R(T1). ✓ from IH. The arrow relation's semantic clause quantifies over values a with R(T1)(a), which is fine regardless of rho/σ_ty. The All case uses lc of σ_ty U. The Abs case uses lc of σ_ty T1. So no tie needed! The point is that R interprets types semantically via rho, independent of the syntactic σ_ty; the fact that the substituted term has type σ_ty T is not needed for relatedness. Interesting. Is the theorem actually TRUE then? Let's sanity check the T_Var case: env gives R rho [] V (σt x) ✓. The conclusion is R rho [] V (σ_tm x) = same ✓. Good.

Let's check the TAbs case in detail with this statement:
- Goal: R rho [] (Ty_All T1) (subst (tm_tabs s)) = R(rho,[]) (All T1) (tm_tabs (subst_ty s? and term subst)).
  R(All T1)(tabs s') = ∃v0, tabs s' -->* v0 ∧ value v0 ∧ V(All T1)(v0).
  Take v0 = tabs s'; multi_refl; value requires lc_tm_at 0 0 (tm_tabs s'). Need lc of the substituted term.
  V(All T1)(tabs s') = ∀U R0, lc_ty_at 0 U → R(rho, R0::[]) T1 (tapp (tabs s') U).
  For arbitrary U, R0, lc U: by ST_TAppTabs (needs lc (tabs s') ✓, lc U ✓), tapp (tabs s') U --> open_tm_ty s' U.
  Need R(rho, R0::[]) T1 (open_tm_ty s' U) = ∃w, open_tm_ty s' U -->* w ∧ value w ∧ V(rho,R0::[]) T1 w.
  IH: for the premise. The premise's typing context is (X::Δ), and the term is open_tm_ty s X, type open_ty T1 X. We need to instantiate the IH with:
    σ_ty' := σ_ty[X ↦ U],

### Part 1: Basic list/nat lemmas
- nth_error/nth lemmas for the level env. Let me use `nth` with default: `nth i xi default`? Coq's `nth` requires a default. I'll define a helper: 
  ```
  Fixpoint lookup_level (xi : list sem) (i : nat) : sem :=
    match xi, i with
    | P :: _, 0 => P
    | _ :: xi', S i' => lookup_level xi' i'
    | [], _ => fun _ => False
    end.
  ```
  Then prove lookup_level_app etc. as needed. The open_ty_rel lemma needs: lookup_level (R0 :: xi) 0 = R0, lookup_level (R0::xi) (S i) = lookup_level xi i. Definitional ✓.

### Part 2: fv functions
```
Fixpoint fv_ty (T : ty) : list atom :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar _ => []
  | Ty_FVar X => [X]
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => fv_ty T1 ++ fv_ty T2
  | Ty_All T1 => fv_ty T1
  end.

Fixpoint fv_tm_ty (t : tm) : list atom := (* type atoms in annotations *)
  match t with
  | tm_abs T t1 => fv_ty T ++ fv_tm_ty t1
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fv_tm_ty t1 ++ fv_tm_ty t2
  | tm_tabs t1 => fv_tm_ty t1
  | tm_tapp t1 T => fv_tm_ty t1 ++ fv_ty T
  | tm_choice t1 t2 => fv_tm_ty t1 ++ fv_tm_ty t2
  | _ => []
  end.

Fixpoint fv_tm (t : tm) : list atom := (* free term variable atoms *)
  match t with
  | tm_fvar x => [x]
  | tm_abs T t1 => fv_tm t1
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fv_tm t1 ++ fv_tm t2
  | tm_tabs t1 => fv_tm t1
  | tm_tapp t1 T => fv_tm t1
  | tm_choice t1 t2 => fv_tm t1 ++ fv_tm t2
  | tm_bvar _ => []
  end.

Fixpoint fv_ctx (Gamma : context) : list atom :=
  match Gamma with
  | [] => []
  | (x,T) :: G => x :: fv_ty T ++ fv_ctx G
  end.
```

Fresh:
```
Fixpoint max_list (l : list nat) : nat :=
  match l with [] => 0 | x :: l' => max x (max_list l') end.
Lemma max_list_spec : forall l x, In x l -> x <= max_list l.
Definition fresh (l : list nat) : nat := S (max_list l).
Lemma fresh_not_in : forall l, ~ In (fresh l) l.
```

### Part 3: lc_ty_at lemmas
- `lc_ty_at_mono : forall k1 k2 T, k1 <= k2 -> lc_ty_at k1 T -> lc_ty_at k2 T` — probably provable by induction on the lc derivation or on T.
- For wf_ty → lc_ty_at 0: need a lemma about open_ty and lc.

Let me think about how to prove wf_ty Δ T → lc_ty_at 0 T. We need: WF_All case: ∀X∉L, wf_ty (X::Δ) (open_ty T X) → (by IH) ∀X∉L, lc_ty_at 0 (open_ty T X) → lc_ty_at 1 T. 

Prove a general lemma: if lc_ty_at 0 (open_ty_rec k (Ty_FVar X) T) and ... then lc_ty_at (S? k?) T. Let's think carefully. Define the statement:

`lc_ty_open_inv : forall k T X, lc_ty_at k (open_ty_rec k (Ty_FVar X) T) -> lc_ty_at (S k) T`? Hmm, wait, the ambient depth: open_ty_rec k U T is interpreted with U at level k. If the opened type is lc at level k (all free BVars < k), then what about T? T's free BVars: those < k (not replaced) and those = k (replaced by FVar X, which is not a BVar) and those > k? Hmm, if T has BVar j with j > k, it's not replaced and remains free in open_ty_rec, violating lc at k. So T's free BVars are < k or = k, i.e., < S k. So lc_ty_at (S k) T ✓. But wait, under binders inside T, indices shift. Let's prove by induction on T with a generalization over k:

Claim: forall T k X, lc_ty_at k (open_ty_rec k (Ty_FVar X) T) -> lc_ty_at (S k) T.
Proof by induction on T; generalizing k.
- Ty_BVar i: open = if i =? k then FVar X else BVar i. The premise lc_ty_at k (result). If i = k: result FVar X → lc_ty_at k (FVar X) always ✓; need lc_ty_at (S k) (BVar i) = i < S k, i.e., i ≤ k ✓ (i = k). If i ≠ k: result BVar i with lc_ty_at k → i < k → i < S k ✓.
- Ty_FVar ✓.
- Arrow: premise gives lc of components at k; IH ✓.
- All T1: open_ty_rec k (FVar X) (Ty_All T1) = Ty_All (open_ty_rec (S k) (FVar X) T1). Premise lc_ty_at k (Ty_All ...) means lc_ty_at (S k) (open_ty_rec (S k) (FVar X) T1). IH on T1 with k := S k gives lc_ty_at (S (S k)) T1. Then lc_ty_at (S k) (Ty_All T1) ✓.
✓.

For WF_All: the premise gives lc_ty_at 0 (open_ty T X) where open_ty T X = open_ty_rec 0 (FVar X) T. Apply the claim with k=0: lc_ty_at 1 T. Then lc_ty_at 0 (Ty_All T) ✓.

Also need: `lc_ty_at` for types substituted? Let's list the needed lc_ty lemmas:
- wf_ty Δ T → lc_ty_at 0 T.
- lc_ty_at 0 (ty_subst_all σ T) given lc_ty_at 0 T and ∀X, lc_ty_at 0 (σ X). Actually we may need a general statement at level k. Let's prove: forall k T σ, lc_ty_at k T -> (forall X, In X (fv_ty T) -> lc_ty_at k (σ X)) -> lc_ty_at k (ty_subst_all σ T). Since substitution of a closed type at level k preserves lc at level k? If σ X is lc at level k and the substitution happens under type binders, the indices... σ X is closed, so fine under deeper binders; lc at level k for a closed type holds at any level (monotonicity). We may need lc at level k+j. Hmm, let's state: if lc_ty_at 0 (σ X) for all X, and lc_ty_at k T, then lc_ty_at k (ty_subst_all σ T). Under Ty_All, T1 at level S k, and the σ's are lc at 0 hence at S k (monotonicity) ✓. So the proof works with the global hypothesis ∀X, lc_ty_at 0 (σ X).

When we open a term at U (open_tm_ty), we replace the innermost type binder (index 0 in the term's annotations, or index k for the k-th enclosing binder). For the *result* to be lc at K where the opened binder is removed... indices referring to *outer* binders: in the original term under k type binders, an annotation's index j < k refers to an outer binder; after opening the k-th binder (the innermost of the opened scope), indices j < k still refer to the same outer binders, and the check at level K should be j < K? Hmm, I think I need to think about what lc_tm_at's K means precisely.

`lc_tm_at K k t`: K is the number of enclosing *type* binders (tabs) in the term, and k the number of enclosing *term* binders. For annotations inside t at type-binder depth d (relative to the current point), the type is checked at lc_ty_at (K + d)? Let's look at the rules:
- lc_tm_abs: lc_tm_at K k (tm_abs T t) requires lc_ty_at K T and lc_tm_at K (S k) t. So the annotation T at the current point is checked at K.
- lc_tm_tabs: lc_tm_at K k (tm_tabs t) requires lc_tm_at (S K) k t.
- lc_tm_tapp: lc_ty_at K T (the type argument at the current point).
So K counts enclosing tabs. The type BVar indices in annotations refer to enclosing tabs? But wait: the term `tm_tabs t` has its type variable available inside t; how do annotations inside t refer to it? In the T_TAbs typing rule, the body term is opened via `open_tm_ty t (Ty_FVar X)`, which replaces the type BVar index 0 in annotations with FVar X. So under one tabs, annotations' index 0 refers to that tabs. Under another nested tabs, index 0 refers to the inner one and index 1 to the outer. So at a point with K enclosing tabs, an annotation T checked with lc_ty_at K allows BVars < K, i.e., indices referring to any of the K enclosing tabs ✓. 

Now, opening `open_tm_ty_rec k U t` where k is the number of type binders to skip? Wait, the definition:
```
Fixpoint open_tm_ty_rec (k : nat) (U : ty) (t : tm) :=
  match t with
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs (open_ty_rec k U T) (open_tm_ty_rec k U t1)
  | tm_tabs t1 => tm_tabs (open_tm_ty_rec (S k) U t1)
  | tm_tapp t1 T => tm_tapp (...) (open_ty_rec k U T)
  ...
```
So open_tm_ty (t, U) = open_tm_ty_rec 0 U t replaces index 0 in annotations, and under tm_tabs, index S k. This is the standard "open the outermost type binder" operation. For a term t = tm_tabs t1 (the body of a type abstraction), open_tm_ty t U opens the binder corresponding to the tabs.

Now the lc relationship: If `lc_tm_at K k (open_tm_ty t (Ty_FVar X))` and X is fresh, what can we say about `lc_tm_at (S K) k t`? Hmm, open_tm_ty t X replaces index 0 with FVar X in annotations at the top level, and under k inner tabs replaces index k with FVar X. The result is lc at K. The original t was under one more binder (the opened one): annotations at the top level have the opened binder as index 0; after opening, the index 0 is replaced. Higher indices j > 0 in the original refer to... the opened binder is index 0 only at the top level; after opening, the remaining indices j (j ≥ 1) in the original refer to outer binders beyond the opened one, but for the opened term to be lc at K, we need them < K? This is confusing because the de Bruijn convention here doesn't decrement indices: `open_ty_rec k U (Ty_BVar j) = if j = k then U else Ty_BVar j`. If the type binder at level k is *removed*, then indices above k should decrement. But this open_ty is used in the typing rule `open_ty T U` where T is the *body* of `Ty_All T` (the binder is the All itself in the type syntax, which remains as `Ty_All` around the body). When instantiating, we're not removing the binder from the surrounding context because the surrounding context is outside the type; the opened type is a new type in the outer context. Hmm, but the outer context's binders are at levels... Let me think of the type `Ty_All T` in context Δ. T's BVar 0 = the All's binder; BVar j (j>0) = the (j-1)-th enclosing binder in Δ. Wait no: BVar indices count binders from the innermost. In T (inside the All), the enclosing binders are: the All (index 0) and then the enclosing binders of the whole type? But the enclosing binders of a type in context Δ are free atoms (Ty_FVar), not BVars — unless the type is itself under another All inside the syntax. So in `Ty_All T` as a type, T's BVar indices: 0 = this All; j>0 = an enclosing All within the same type syntax (i.e., if `Ty_All T` is itself the body of another All). In a well-formed type, all the enclosing binders are syntactic Alls of the type itself, so the indices are all within the type syntax; free variables are Ty_FVar atoms. ✓.

So when we open `Ty_All T` at U: `open_ty (Ty_All T) U = Ty_All (open_ty_rec 1 U T)`. Wait, why 1? The type `Ty_All T` is being opened at its *outer* binder, which corresponds to index... Hmm, `open_ty T U = open_ty_rec 0 U T` replaces BVar 0 in T. But `Ty_All T`'s own binder is the one we want to open, and in T it's index 0. But `open_ty (Ty_All T) U = Ty_All (open_ty_rec 0 U T)`, since the recursion under Ty_All makes it open_ty_rec (S 0)=1 on T. That's wrong! Let me recheck the code:

```
Fixpoint open_ty_rec (k : nat) (U T : ty) : ty :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => if Nat.eqb k i then U else Ty_BVar i
  ...
  | Ty_All T1 => Ty_All (open_ty_rec (S k) U T1)
  end.
```
So open_ty_rec 0 U (Ty_All T1) = Ty_All (open_ty_rec 1 U T1). This opens the *wrong* variable: it opens index 1 inside T1, not index 0 (which is the All's own binder). Hmm, but maybe this is intended for opening a type under an additional *outer* binder: open_ty_rec k U T replaces the free variable with index k, where k counts the binders *above* T (outside T). So `open_ty T U` with T having a free index... but T is a type with a free BVar? In the typing rule T_TAbs, `open_ty T (Ty_FVar X)` where T is the body of Ty_All T. No wait, the rule uses `open_ty T X` where T is the body! `has_type Delta Gamma (tm_tabs t) (Ty_All T)` with premise `has_type (X::Delta) Gamma (open_tm_ty t (Ty_FVar X)) (open_ty T (Ty_FVar X))`. So the body T is opened directly with open_ty T X = open_ty_rec 0 X T, which replaces the body's BVar 0 = the All's binder ✓. Yes! And in T_TApp, the type is `open_ty T U` where T is the body of Ty_All T ✓. So open_ty is always applied to the *body* of an All, replacing its index 0 (the All's binder). Under nested Alls within the body, indices shift, and `open_ty_rec` increments k under Ty_All. E.g., body T = Ty_All T2 (the type ∀Y.T2; here index 0 in T2 refers to Y, index 1 refers to the outer All's binder). Then open_ty T U = Ty_All (open_ty_rec 1 U T2) replaces index 1 in T2 with U ✓. Correct.

So `open_ty_rec k U T` replaces BVar k with U, where k counts the type binders *inside* T above the current point (i.e., the opened binder is k levels up). The type U is in the *outer* context (where the opened binder doesn't exist), which is why no decrementing is needed: U's free type variables are Ty_FVar atoms or bound by binders outside the type syntax (impossible since types are self-contained, so U's binders are internal Alls). ✓.

Now, lc: `open_ty_rec k U T` where T is lc at level n (all BVars < n) and U is lc at level n' — the result's BVars: those < n except k, replaced by U's BVars (U is lc at 0, so none free). No—T is the direct body of Ty_All; the only binder enclosing T is this All, which is index 0. So T has no free BVars > 0 at the top level (in a well-formed type). Under inner Alls of T, indices shift, but those refer to inner binders first. So in a *well-formed* type, after opening index 0, the result has no free BVars at the top level. That means opening is well-behaved with lc at level 0.

So for the lc lemmas, the relevant statement is probably: if lc_ty_at k T (all free BVars < k) and U lc at 0, then lc_ty_at k (open_ty_rec j U T) provided j < k? Now the reverse direction (needed for wf_ty→lc and body lemmas): `lc_ty_at k (open_ty_rec k (Ty_FVar X) T) → lc_ty_at (S k) T` — proven above ✓.

For terms:

- lc_tm_at and open_tm_ty: We need to understand the lc relation for type opening in terms. Type annotations inside a term under K enclosing tabs are checked at lc_ty_at K. Opening a type binder: `open_tm_ty_rec k U t` replaces type index k in annotations. Here the level is K and the index is k, and they might differ! In lc_tm_at K k t, the K is the ambient type level and open_tm_ty_rec k uses the index k (which equals the term's term-binder level, not the type level). Ugh, the parameters: lc_tm_at K k t where K = type binders and k = term binders. open_tm_ty_rec k U t uses k = term-binder count?? The notation is confusing: open_tm_ty_rec k with k counting tabs. In the term body of a tabs, when we call open_tm_ty t U = open_tm_ty_rec 0 U t, the 0 is the type binder count. So in the lc lemma, we'd have lc_tm_at K k (open_tm_ty_rec j U t) where j counts type binders, and lc_tm_at K k has K type binders and k term binders. The type-opening index j should be related to the type level K, not the term level k. Hmm, but the open_tm_ty_rec traversal under tm_abs keeps j fixed and under tm_tabs increments. And lc_tm_at under tm_abs increments the term level k, and under tm_tabs increments K. So they're independent parameters: j (type-opening index) vs K (type level). In the body lemma for T_TAbs, the opened term open_tm_ty_rec 0 X s is lc at K=0 (if typing at top level), and we want s lc at K=1. Hmm, defining FSubst as a composition might require commuting the two substitutions through the term structure. Let me define a *single* combined substitution function:

```
Fixpoint subst_all (sigma_ty : atom -> ty) (sigma_tm : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma_tm x
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs (ty_subst_all sigma_ty T) (subst_all sigma_ty sigma_tm t1)
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst_all ... t1) (subst_all ... t2)
  | tm_tabs t1 => tm_tabs (subst_all ... t1)
  | tm_tapp t1 T => tm_tapp (subst_all ... t1) (ty_subst_all sigma_ty T)
  | tm_choice t1 t2 => tm_choice (subst_all ... t1) (subst_all ... t2)
  end.
```
and
```
Fixpoint ty_subst_all (sigma_ty : atom -> ty) (T : ty) : ty :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => Ty_BVar i
  | Ty_FVar X => sigma_ty X
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => Ty_Arrow (ty_subst_all sigma_ty T1) (ty_subst_all sigma_ty T2)
  | Ty_All T1 => Ty_All (ty_subst_all sigma_ty T1)
  end.
```
Note: ty_subst_all under Ty_All doesn't shift indices; since σ types are lc (no free BVars), capture isn't an issue? Hmm, actually if σ X contains free BVars, substituting under a binder would capture; but we require lc. However, there's another issue: the body of Ty_All contains BVar 0 bound by the All; the substitution only replaces FVars, so BVars are untouched ✓. And σ X lc → its BVars are bound internally ✓.

With this single combined substitution, the commutation lemmas are cleaner:
- subst_all over open_tm: subst_all σ_ty σ_tm (open_tm t (fvar x)) with σ_tm' = σ_tm[x↦a]... hmm, since subst_all is combined, we can prove:

A type atom could numerically equal the term atom x. The substitution σ_ty maps type atoms; fv_tm_ty tracks type atoms in annotations. The term substitution σ_tm maps term atoms. Since the namespaces are the same nat, a free type atom Y with Y = x would be substituted by σ_ty Y (type substitution), and a free term variable x by σ_tm x. They're different substitutions, applied to different occurrences; no conflict. But in the commutation lemma, we need to ensure that the binding variable x (a term variable) doesn't occur as a *type* atom in annotations in a way that matters? The type substitution σ_ty is the same on both sides, and type atoms aren't affected by term opening. So no issue ✓.

**The rho/V extension lemmas.**

We need:
1. `V_ext : (forall X, In X (fv_ty T) -> forall t, rho1 X t <-> rho2 X t) -> forall t, V rho1 xi T t <-> V rho2 xi T t`. Actually, since rho1 X and rho2 X are predicates, the natural hypothesis is `rho1 X = rho2 X` pointwise. Let's state with `forall X, In X (fv_ty T) -> forall t, rho1 X t <-> rho2 X t`, or use pointwise equality `forall X, In X (fv_ty T) -> rho1 X = rho2 X` — function equality requires functional extensionality if we want to rewrite, but we can just have the pointwise iff and prove V pointwise iff.

   - FVar: apply H with the fv hypothesis (In X (fv_ty (Ty_FVar X)) = In X [X] ✓).

   - All: the All clause quantifies over R0 (predicates) and uses rho with the level env R0::xi; the IH at the same rho's with the level env R0::xi applies since rho doesn't change ✓.
